Q.

6

7

8

9

10 
(“Niagara Mohawk” or the “Company”). 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please describe your educational background and experience. 
13 
A.

14 
Science in Geology.  From 1981 to 1989, I worked as a Research Associate with Jensen 
15 
Associates, Inc. of Boston where I was responsible for developing a variety of computer 
16 
forecasting models to analyze natural gas supply and demand for interstate pipeline and 
17 
local distribution companies.  Upon accepting employment with Boston Gas Company in 
18 
1989, I was responsible for modeling and forecasting the natural gas resource requirements 
19 
of the company’s customers and managing its resource-planning process.  In 1998 and 
20 
1999, I undertook the same responsibility for Essex Gas Company and Colonial Gas 
21 
Company, respectively.  In 2000, I assumed responsibility for modeling and forecasting 
22 
the natural gas resource requirements of The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National

II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
16 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony? 
17 
A. 
The purpose of my testimony is to provide historic and forecast customer and customer 
18 
demand data used by the Company to prepare the forecast of gas revenues and gas costs 
19 
for the twelve months ending June 30, 2022 (“Rate Year”).  The data includes historic data 
20 
through February 2020 and forecast data for the Rate Year and the following two years 
21 
ending June 30, 2023, and June 30, 2024 (“Data Year 1” and “Data Year 2,” respectively).

III. 
Overview of the Company’s Retail Gas Market 
2 
Q. 
What were the Company’s historic gas deliveries from FY 2016 through FY 2020 as 
3 
shown in Exhibit __ (TEP-1)? 
4 
A. 
Actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 1.611 billion therms to 1.786 billion therms 
5 
to all customers (Exhibit ___ (TEP-1)) from FY 2016 through FY 2020.1  Retail refers to 
6 
gas delivered and metered at the customers’ burner tips, while wholesale refers to metered 
7 
gas received and flowing into the Company’s distribution system.  The range in volumes 
8 
of actual retail gas deliveries was primarily driven by fluctuations in the weather, such as 
9 
the abnormally warm winters of 2015-2016 and 2016-2017.   
10 
 
11 
On a weather-normalized basis, retail gas deliveries to all customers have declined at a 
12 
compound annual growth rate of -2.7 percent per annum from 1.877 billion therms in FY 
13 
2016 to 1.686 billion therms in FY 2020 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8)).  This decline reflects 
14 
changes in the residential and commercial markets and fluctuations in the large-volume 
15 
markets.  Historic actual billed sales by month and by class for FYs 2016 to 2020 are 
16 
                                                 
1 Fiscal year runs from April through March, e.g., FY 2016 would encompass the period April 2015 through March 
2016. 


--- Page 7 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 5 of 22 
 
provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-7).  Historic weather-normalized billed sales by month and 
1 
by class for FYs 2016 to 2020 are provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-8). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What was the Company’s historic number of customers from FY 2016 through FY 
4 
2020, as shown in Exhibit _ (TEP-3)? 
5 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-3), the total number of customers has grown at a compound 
6 
average net growth rate of 0.9 percent from 610,158 at the end of FY 2016 to 633,472 at 
7 
the end of FY 2020, or an average annual addition of 5,828 customers per year.  Historic 
8 
customer counts by month and by class are provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-9).

IV. 
Gas Delivery Forecast 
11 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s individual service and revenue classes. 
12 
A. 
The Company’s tariff (PSC No. 219 Gas) includes the following service classifications: 
13 
SC1, SC2, SC3, SC4, SC5, SC6, SC7, SC8, SC9, SC10, SC12, SC13, and SC14.  The 
14 
Company also has an SC11 service class that sets forth the terms and conditions that apply 
15 
to other service classes for load aggregation and balancing services under the Company’s 
16 
retail choice and transportation programs.  
17 
 
18 
Within certain service classifications, there are distinct revenue classes.  These revenue 
19 
classes are Residential Non-Heat, Residential Heat, Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial 
20 
Heat, and Industrial.  For these revenue classes, the Company developed forecasts at the 
21 
rate code level.  For example, for the Residential heating class, there are separate forecasts 
22 


--- Page 8 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 6 of 22 
 
for SC1 Residential Heat, SC1 Residential Heat Monthly Balancing, SC2 Residential Heat, 
1 
and SC2 Residential Heat Monthly Balancing.   
2 
 
3 
In addition to the distinct revenue classes named above, there are five other special 
4 
categories that are forecast:  deliveries to New York State Electric and Gas Corporation 
5 
(“NYSEG”) under the Mechanicsville SC9 contract, sales of natural gas for vehicular use 
6 
under SC10, SC12 distributed generation (“DG”) sales, SC13 sales to Residential DG 
7 
customers, and SC14 transportation.  
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company prepared its gas delivery forecast. 
10 
A. 
The Company prepared its gas delivery forecast using econometric/statistical forecast 
11 
models developed for two geographic regions (“East Gate” and “West Gate”) and six major 
12 
groupings of its rate classes (“Customer Groups”).  The Company’s West Gate region is 
13 
Amsterdam and the area west of Amsterdam, including Syracuse and Watertown.  The 
14 
Company’s East Gate region is the area east of Amsterdam, as well as municipalities such 
15 
as Albany, Schenectady, and Troy.  The forecasts were based on historic billing data from 
16 
March 2007 through February 2020.  Two different models were used for each 
17 
combination:  (1) a model to forecast the number of customers; and (2) a model to forecast 
18 
use-per-customer.   
19 
 
20 
In the number of customer (meter count) models, the independent variables are a 
21 
combination of time trends and economic variables such as population, employment, gross 
22 
domestic product (“GDP”), and natural gas-to-oil price ratios.  In the models calculating 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 7 of 22 
 
use-per-customer, the independent variables are a combination of time trends, heating 
1 
degree days, and economic variables such as GDP, personal income, and natural gas price.   
2 
 
3 
The Company performed econometric forecasts of meter counts and use-per-customer at 
4 
the Customer Group level and then allocated these values to the Company’s 47 internal 
5 
rate codes that cover its 13 Service Classifications (SC1 - SC14, there being no customers 
6 
in the SC11 class).  The Company then multiplied meter counts and uses-per-customer to 
7 
derive its volume forecast.   
8 
 
9 
For econometric regression analysis, the Company used monthly historic division-specific 
10 
economic and gas and oil price data from Moody’s (publication date: April 2020) and the 
11 
U.S. Department of Energy (“DOE”), Energy Information Administration “EIA”).  The 
12 
independent variables tested for modeling include: 
13 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 8 of 22

1

2 
For weather data, the Company used heating degree day (“HDD”) data for the Albany 
3 
(“KALB”) and Syracuse (“KSYR”) weather stations provided by its weather services 
4 
vendor, Weather Services International. 
5 
 
6 
The Company performed a two-level hierarchical econometric forecast of meter counts and 
7 
use-per-customer to derive its volume forecast.  For each geographic region, the Company 
8 
analyzed meter count and use-per-customer for five major retail Customer Groups: 
9 


--- Page 11 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 9 of 22 
 
Table 2 – Customer Groups 
1 
Customer Group 
Service Classification(s) 
Residential Non-heating 
All SC1 and SC2 Residential data for 
non-spaceheating customers 
Residential Heating     
All SC1 and SC2 Residential data for 
spaceheating customers 
Commercial 
Commercial customers in SC2, SC3, and 
SC7 
Industrial 
Industrial customers in SC2 and SC3 
Other     
Customers in other Service 
Classifications 
 
2 
The Company’s forecasting process begins with modeling the Customer Groups using the 
3 
R programming language as this allows the Company to develop its models and perform 
4 
appropriate statistical tests on them.  Once the models for East Gate and West Gate are 
5 
completed, all of the results are assembled in one Excel spreadsheet to perform the 
6 
remaining parts of developing the forecast. 
7 
 
8 
Although the models are all developed in the Company’s Excel spreadsheet, it was 
9 
necessary for the Company to perform one other step outside of Excel.  The Company used 
10 
the “stl” package in the R programming language to disaggregate its Customer Group-level 
11 
time series meter count data into seasonal, trend, and residual components using LOESS 
12 
regression analysis.  This approach is used because the monthly Company meter count data 
13 
contains both short-term oscillations as well as long-term trends, and the Company’s 
14 
forecast began by testing econometric models of the long-term trends from the “stl” 
15 
analysis.  If the trend models were successful, then seasonality is restored to the forecast 
16 
data from the seasonality component of the “stl” analysis. 
17 
 
18 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 10 of 22 
 
After completing its Customer Group-level modeling and forecasting of meter count and 
1 
use-per-customer, the Company performed a disaggregation of its Customer Group-level 
2 
forecasts to the level of its internal rate codes (leaf-level).  This leaf-level forecast allows 
3 
the Company to aggregate the data to the Service Classification level for presentation and 
4 
for use in its associated wholesale forecast for each geographic region. The total gas 
5 
delivery forecast is the result of customer count forecasts multiplied by use-per-customer 
6 
forecasts that are modeled separately. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the customer count forecast. 
9 
A. 
The Company forecasts meter counts using linear regression analysis of the trend 
10 
component of historical meter counts as a function of economic and demographic variables.  
11 
This provides meter count forecasts for the five Customer Groups reflecting the long-term 
12 
trends. 
13 
 
14 
For its Residential meter count regression analyses, the Company tested the employment, 
15 
GDP, household/population, personal income, price (gas and oil), and time variables to 
16 
choose the most appropriate predictive variable(s).  For all other meter count regression 
17 
analyses, the Company tested the employment, GDP, household/population, retail sales, 
18 
personal income, price (gas and oil), and time variables to choose the most appropriate 
19 
predictive variable(s).   
20 
 
21 
The monthly variation of the seasonal component was then added to the forecast meter 
22 
count trend.  The monthly results of these meter count forecasts were then allocated to the 
23 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 11 of 22 
 
internal rate code level by allocating the Customer Group level meter count to the internal 
1 
rate codes that belong to the Customer Group based on the most recent ratio of each rate 
2 
code to its Customer Group.  The meter count forecast was then converted to a customer 
3 
count forecast based on the average number of meters per customer for each internal rate 
4 
code. 
5 
 
6 
The meter count models for each Customer Group and the data used to derive them are 
7 
described in more detail below.  Details regarding the meter count modeling are provided 
8 
in Exhibit ___ (TEP-10A). 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the derivation of the use-per-customer forecast. 
11 
A. 
The historic monthly use-per-customer values are obtained by dividing the total billed 
12 
therms for each month by the number of billed meters for the month.   
13 
 
14 
The Company’s use-per-customer data for the five Customer Groups contain both long-
15 
term trends and annual variability that reflect the seasonality of usage.  In modeling use 
16 
per customer, the Company found that its use-per-customer data is primarily a function of 
17 
weather.  Other independent variables (e.g., economic and demographic variables, natural 
18 
gas prices) were then tested for goodness-of-fit of the equations to the data.   
19 
 
20 
For its Residential use-per-customer regression analysis, the Company tested the 
21 
employment, personal income, gas price, and billing degree day variables to choose the 
22 
most appropriate predictive variable(s).  For all other use-per-customer regression analysis, 
23 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 12 of 22 
 
the Company tested the employment, GDP, personal income, retail sales, gas price, and 
1 
billing degree day variables to choose the most appropriate predictive variable(s).   
2 
 
3 
Once forecasts of use-per-customer at the Customer Group level were determined, the 
4 
Company multiplied each by its customer count forecast to determine its volume forecast 
5 
by Customer Group.  The monthly results of these volume forecasts were then allocated to 
6 
the internal rate code level by allocating the Customer Group level volume to the internal 
7 
rate codes that belong to the Customer Group based on the most recent percentages of each 
8 
rate code to its Customer Group.  The monthly results of these use-per-customer forecasts 
9 
were then calculated from the rate-code-level forecasts of customer count and volume. 
10 
 
11 
The use-per-customer models for each Customer Group and the data used to derive them 
12 
are described in more detail below.  Details regarding the use-per-customer modeling are 
13 
provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-10B). 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How is the weather variable in the models determined? 
16 
A. 
Weather is a fundamental variable in modeling the natural gas requirements of the 
17 
Company’s customers.  The Company uses gas day (10am – 9am) HDD based on 
18 
temperature observations from two weather stations:  KSYR (Syracuse NY) and KALB 
19 
(Albany NY).  For its East Gate modeling, the Company used the Albany NY weather 
20 
station.  For its West Gate modeling, the Company used the Syracuse NY weather station.  
21 
For its forecast, as shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-11), the Company defined its normal HDD 
22 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 13 of 22 
 
based on a 30-year average of the period from calendar years 1989 - 2018 as 6,410 HDD 
1 
for Albany NY and 6,506 HDD for Syracuse NY. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company adjusted the initial forecast to reflect additional 
4 
efficiency gains resulting from the energy efficiency programs sponsored by the 
5 
Company and the New York State Energy and Research Development Authority 
6 
(“NYSERDA”). 
7 
A. 
During the development of the Company’s gas load forecast, the State of New York Public 
8 
Service Commission issued its “Order Authorizing Utility Energy Efficiency and Building 
9 
Electrification Portfolios through 2025” in Case 18-M-0084.  Because the Company’s 
10 
forecast is one of net natural gas requirements of its customers and because the Company’s 
11 
historic sales data includes the impact of its past actual energy efficiency savings from both 
12 
NYSERDA- and Company-sponsored energy efficiency programs, the Company reduced 
13 
its gas load forecast for only the amount of energy efficiency (its own Enhanced Energy 
14 
Efficiency as well and the New Efficiency: New York programs) reductions above the level 
15 
of its historical claimed savings so as to not double-count reductions.  Additionally, the 
16 
Company further reduced its forecast of customer count and volume to account for the 
17 
impact of the electrification of heat in the residential market. 
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Please explain how the forecasts were adjusted for initiatives in the Company’s 
20 
service territory that could impact the amount of natural gas consumed during the 
21 
Rate Year and Data Years. 
22 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 14 of 22 
 
A. 
In addition to post-forecasting adjustments made to reflect the expansion of the Company’s 
1 
energy efficiency efforts and the impact of electrification of heat, the Company’s forecast 
2 
also reflects three additional adjustments.  The first reflects the proposal to transfer existing 
3 
SC3 customers to other service classifications.  The second reflects the proposal to require 
4 
all new SC5, SC7, or SC8 daily-balanced transportation customers to join the system as 
5 
capacity-eligible customers.  The third reflects a reassignment of certain SC5 and SC7 
6 
customers to service classes more appropriate to their usage. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the annual forecast deliveries and the year-end customer count 
9 
forecast. 
10 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-4), the Company’s forecast of normalized retail gas 
11 
deliveries is 1.641 billion therms in FY 2021, rising to 1.685 billion therms in FY 2026.  
12 
Natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 8.847 million therms per year or a 
13 
compound average annual growth rate of 0.5 percent per annum. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please summarize Moody’s economic forecast for Upstate New York, which underlies 
16 
the economic delivery forecast. 
17 
A. 
The April 2020 economic forecast used by the Company includes the impacts of the 
18 
COVID-19 pandemic on the Upstate New York economy, including both New York Pause 
19 
and the subsequent recession.  For Niagara Mohawk’s East Gate region, population is 
20 
projected to increase by 0.17 percent per annum over the period FY 2021-2026, slightly 
21 
higher than the -0.01 percent per annum growth rate over the period FY 2016-2020.  The 
22 
number of households is projected to increase by 0.36 percent per annum over the period 
23 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 15 of 22 
 
FY 2021-2026, slightly lower than the 0.51 percent per annum growth rate over the period 
1 
FY 2016-2020.  The real per capita personal income is projected to increase by 0.52 percent 
2 
per annum over the period FY 2021-2026, slightly lower than the 1.17 percent per annum 
3 
growth rate over the period FY 2016-2020.  After declining 5.38 percent per annum over 
4 
the period FY 2016-2020, the residential natural gas-to-oil price ratio is forecast to decline 
5 
by 3.09 percent per annum over the period FY 2021-2026 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-6A)). 
6 
 
7 
For Niagara Mohawk’s West Gate, population is projected to decrease by 0.19 percent per 
8 
annum over the period FY 2021-2026, a slightly lower decline than the 0.40 percent per 
9 
annum negative growth rate over the period FY 2016-2020.  The number of households is 
10 
projected to increase by 0.12 percent per annum over the period FY 2021-2026, slightly 
11 
lower than the 0.21 percent per annum growth rate over the period FY 2016-2020.  The 
12 
real per capita personal income is projected to increase by 0.61 percent per annum over the 
13 
period FY 2021-2026, slightly lower than the 1.39 percent per annum growth rate over the 
14 
period FY 2015-2019.  After declining 5.38 percent per annum over the period FY 2016-
15 
2020, the residential natural gas-to-oil price ratio is forecast to decline by 3.09 percent per 
16 
annum over the period FY 2021-2026 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-6B)). 
17 
 
18 
As National Grid’s efforts to assess the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic proceed, the 
19 
Company will refine and update the projections noted above, as appropriate.

V. 
Modeling and Forecast Summary 
1 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company modeled the Residential Non-Heating customer 
2 
counts and Dekatherms (“Dth”). 
3 
A. 
The Residential Non-Heating class comprises the Company’s SC1 and SC2 Residential 
4 
non-heating customers, along with its SC13 Residential Distributed Generation. 
5 
 
6 
Residential Non-Heating Meter Counts 
7 
The Residential Non-Heating class accounted for 26,460 customers at year end FY 2020, 
8 
or 4.2 percent of total customers (Exhibit ___ (TEP-3)).  The Residential Non-Heating 
9 
class is projected to decrease to 19,386 customers at year end FY 2026 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-
10 
5)).  Annual meter counts for the Residential Non-Heating class were modeled as functions 
11 
of time and oil price in the East Gate and as a function of time in the West Gate.   
12 
 
13 
Residential Non-Heating Use per Customer 
14 
Residential Non-Heating normalized use-per-customer was 164 therms per year in FY 
15 
2020 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-13)).  By FY 2026, Residential Non-Heating normalized use-per-
16 
customer is projected to decrease to 155 therms per year (Exhibit ___ (TEP-14)).  
17 
Residential Non-Heating use per customer was modeled as a function of billing degree 
18 
days (“BDD”) in both the peak and off-peak periods for the East Gate.  For the West Gate, 
19 
Residential Non-Heating use per customer was modeled as a function of BDD and time in 
20 
the peak period and off-peak period. 
21 
 
22 
Residential Non-Heating Volumes 
23 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 17 of 22 
 
The results of the customer count forecasts and the use-per-customer forecasts were 
1 
multiplied together to derive the volume delivery forecast presented in Exhibit ___ (TEP-
2 
4).  Residential Non-Heating volumes accounted for 0.004 billion therms in FY 2020 on a 
3 
normalized basis, or 0.3 percent of total volume (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8)).  By the end of FY 
4 
2026, Residential Non-Heating volumes are projected to decrease to 0.003 billion therms, 
5 
or 0.2 percent of total volume (Exhibit ___ (TEP-4)).   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please explain how the Company modeled the Residential Heating customer counts 
8 
and Dth. 
9 
A. 
The Residential Heating class comprises the Company’s SC1 and SC2 Residential heating 
10 
customers. 
11 
 
12 
Residential Heating Meter Counts 
13 
The Residential Heating class accounted for 559,103 customers at year end FY 2020, or 
14 
88.3 percent of total customers (Exhibit ___ (TEP-3)).  The Residential Heating class is 
15 
projected to increase to 570,746 customers at year end FY 2026 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-5)).  
16 
Annual meter counts for the Residential Heating class were modeled as functions of 
17 
households in the East Gate and as a function of time and personal income in the West 
18 
Gate.   
19 
 
20 
Residential Heating Use per Customer 
21 
Residential Heating normalized use-per-customer was 1,007 therms per year in FY 2020 
22 
(Exhibit ___ (TEP-13)).  By FY 2026, Residential Heating normalized use-per-customer 
23 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Theodore E. Poe, Jr. 
 
Page 18 of 22 
 
is projected to decrease to 976 therms per year (Exhibit ___ (TEP-14)).  Use-per-customer 
1 
for the Residential Heating classes was modeled as a function of BDD for both the East 
2 
Gate and West Gate.  Because of the high correlation between Residential Heating use-
3 
per-customer and BDD, no other economic variable contributed to improving the model 
4 
equations except for the West Gate off-peak period where time was also a statistically-
5 
significant variable. 
6 
 
7 
Residential Heating Volumes 
8 
The results of the customer count forecasts and the use-per-customer forecasts were 
9 
multiplied together to derive the volume delivery forecast presented in Exhibit ___ (TEP-
10 
4).  Residential Heating volumes accounted for 0.563 billion therms in FY 2020 on a 
11 
normalized basis, or 33.4 percent of total volume (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8)).  By the end of FY 
12 
2026, Residential Heating volumes are projected to increase to 0.557 billion therms, or 
13 
33.1 percent of total volume (Exhibit ___ (TEP-4)).

17

20

21 
of total customers (Exhibit ___ (TEP-3)).  The Commercial class is projected to increase 
22 
to 50,215 customers at year end FY 2026 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-5)).  Annual meter counts for 
23

the Commercial class were modeled as functions of GDP and the gas-to-oil price ratio in 
1 
the East Gate and as a function of manufacturing employment and GDP in the West Gate.   
2

Commercial classes was modeled as a function of BDD and GDP for the East Gate peak 
8

9

10
